2 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
The Five Shocks of 2026—And the Extraordinary Resilience of the U.S. Economy
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
This is the year the consensus got mugged.
Rewind to the start of the year. The market consensus was that the U.S. economy was
set to slow. The Fed was set to cut interest rates. Corporate earnings were set to grow—

but not explode. Energy prices would remain tame. And the odds looked good that the
Republicans would control the House, the Senate, or both, following the midterm election.
Nine months on, the landscape looks starkly different. Defined by five shocks—energy,
interest rates, earnings, growth, and politics—2026 looks nothing like the year investors
thought they were buying on January 1.
Shock 1: Diesel Matters. While global oil prices have moved higher since the start of

the U.S./Iran war on February 28, oil prices have yet to surpass their previous record high
because the world has released some 400 million barrels from strategic reserves this year,
China has curbed its demand for oil imports, and the U.S. has goosed its energy exports to
record highs.
But here is the problem: While gasoline powers the American consumer, diesel powers the
American economy—and diesel prices are now sitting at record highs. Diesel supplies have

been hammered by wars in the Middle East and Ukraine that have sharply reduced refining
capacity. According to the International Energy Agency, by August, exports of diesel from
refiners in the Middle East had fallen by a quarter from prewar levels. Meanwhile, Ukraine
has hit 24 of Russia’s 34 largest refineries, reducing supplies from one of the world’s
largest diesel exporters. The upshot: U.S. diesel prices have soared from $3.55 a gallon
at the start of the year to roughly $6.52 a gallon. While crude oil trades for around $100

a barrel, the retail equivalent of a barrel of diesel now costs more than $260. The diesel
shock of 2026 was the last thing investors saw coming—and remains a key impediment to
structurally lower inflation.
Shock 2: From Interest Rate Cuts to Rate Hikes. Perhaps no other consensus view
entering 2026 has been turned more dramatically on its head than expectations for the
Fed. Heading into 2026, markets were pricing in rate cuts on expectations of a cooling

labor market, moderating inflation, and slower economic growth. The Fed easing cycle was
expected to continue in 2026. In January 2026, futures showed a roughly 50% chance of a
cut by March.
Expectations, however, flipped in late February with the start of the U.S./Iran war. The
Middle East energy shock reignited inflationary pressures, which, combined with stronger-
than-expected U.S. economic growth and a tightening labor market, have demolished the

odds of easier money in 2026. Short- and long-term Treasury yields rose sharply last week
in the wake of the S&P Global Flash U.S. Composite Purchasing Managers’ Index (PMI)
which pointed to the strongest business expansion in more than five years and the fastest
employment growth in over four years, while also reinforcing expectations that the Fed
will maintain a restrictive policy stance for longer. Markets were expecting two to three
quarter-point rate cuts in 2026; instead, the Fed has already raised rates once this year and

is expected to keep raising rates over the near term.
Shock 3: The Earnings Boom No One Saw Coming. Not all the shocks of 2026 are
negative. Indeed, stronger-than-expected corporate earnings have been among the most
positive factors of this year. Corporate America entered the year facing questions about
tariffs, AI-related capital expenditure spending, the health of the U.S. consumer, operating
leverage and the U.S. dollar outlook. At the start of the year, Wall Street was expecting

roughly $290 of S&P 500 earnings per share for 2026, per Bloomberg estimates. But by
September 21, the estimate had risen by $50, to $340 per share.
Investment Implications
While some of these shocks may
contribute to periods of market
volatility, the combination of strong
earnings growth, robust domestic
demand, and ongoing capital
investment suggests that the
fundamental backdrop for Equities
remains constructive.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
Earnings have been upgraded and underpinned by several factors, including robust final 
domestic demand in the U.S., a pickup in global growth, AI-related capital spending, 
productivity gains, and enhanced operating leverage. All these variables have helped deliver 
an earnings “shock” to Wall Street. And as an aside, yes, headline profit growth has been 
inflated by strong technology earnings and one-off investment gains from Amazon and 
Alphabet. That said, the S&P 500’s earnings story remains extraordinary. According to 
FactSet, the 493 companies excluding the Magnificent 7 1 
posted 31.8% earnings growth in 
Q2, the strongest level since late 2021.

Shock 4: An Economy with more Horsepower than the Headlines Suggest. That 
corporate profits have exceeded expectations this ye
ar is due in no small part to an economy 
that continues to surprise to the upside. The Fed was supposed to cut rates this year to 
keep the economy humming and prevent it from losing steam. But rather than limp through 
2026, the economy has leaped ahead. Yes, real gross domestic product (GDP) growth of 
2.1% in Q1 and 1.5% in Q2 are hardly blockbuster annualized rates of growth. However, the 
figures belie the underlying strength of the U.S. economy, with real final domestic demand

(which strips out trade) running at a 4.2% annual rate in Q2. According to the latest reading 
of the Atlanta Fed GDP tracker, Q3 real GDP grow
th is running at a red-hot rate of 5.1%. 
Slowdown? What slowdown?
Resilient consumer spending, robust capital investment, record leve
ls of new business 
formation, and strong foreign direct investment inflows have combined to stoke growth 
in the $32 trillion behemoth called the U.S. economy—the most resilient and dynamic 
economy in the world.
Shock 5: The Unexpected Washington Reset. The last shock is still in the making, but 
given current trends, the odds are rising that the Democrats could control both the House 
and Senate follow
ing the midterm elections. To this point, in January, and per Kalshi, the 
Democrats had a 71% shot at the House and an 18% chance of taking the Senate. Fast-
forward to today, and the odds are a 90% chance that the Democrats take the House and a 
60% chance that they take the Senate.
How all of this plays out remains to be seen—there are some 36 days before the November 
vote, and anything can happen. The market base case remains that the Democrats take the 
House, and the Senate is a toss-up—or too close to call. But with the Democrats expected to 
control at least one chamber of Congress, inv
estors can expect more volatility and uncertainty 
about negotiations ov
er the budget, debt ceiling, defense spending and AI regulation. Another 
political wildcard to watch: the changing dynamics within the Democratic Party.
The Biggest Shock of All for 2026? Pe
rhaps it’s not $6 diesel, rising interest rates, 
solid growth, booming profits or shifting politics. Rather, it’s the ability of the U.S. 
economy to absorb the unexpected, not buckle under stress, and continue generating  wealth 
for investors. To wit, the S&P 500 is on pace for its fourth
 consecutive year of double-digit 
returns.

### Page 4

4 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
2026 Has, So Far, Defied the Traditional Midterm Year Playbook
Jordy Fuentes, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
Current Landscape. To no surprise, market volatility persisted in 2026. Performance initially
tracked historical midterm election patterns before diverging meaningfully as the year
progressed, though there are still three months to go. Several items remain at hand as U.S.

voters head to the polls in November, including geopolitical and foreign policy uncertainty,
energy driven inflation pressures, affordability concerns, U.S. fiscal trajectory, and rapid
advancements in AI. Regardless of the eventual political outcome, history suggests that
markets and the economy can perform well across different configurations of government.
In our view, markets continue to be anchored by strong fundamentals and encouraging
seasonal trends.

To Be Addressed. U.S. domestic and foreign policy remain in focus as the global reorder,
tit-for-tat tariffs, and geopolitical conflicts spanning the Middle East to Eastern Europe have
added layers of uncertainty to the U.S. economic outlook.
At the same time, persistent geopolitical flare-ups have pushed oil prices materially
higher, adding to inflation pressures and keeping them above the Fed’s 2% target.
Headline Consumer Price Index (CPI) held above 3% year-over-year (YoY) in August, while

its core measure, which excludes food and energy, held at 2.4% YoY. The latest Personal
Consumption Expenditure (PCE), the Fed’s preferred measure of inflation, also remained
above 3% YoY for both its headline and core figures. 2
The U.S. fiscal trajectory is another area of focus. The U.S. budget deficit stands at 5.4% of
GDP , just above this century’s average of 4.6%. Meanwhile, U.S. public sector debt surpassed
the $40 trillion mark for the first time in August, oscillating around 100% of GDP .3 While these

trends warrant close monitoring, we view them as a slow-moving structural headwind rather
than an immediate concern, particularly within a highly innovative and diverse U.S. economy.
AI has emerged as a policy consideration, with debates increasingly centered on both
regulatory safeguards and the economic implications of the expanding infrastructure buildout.
For voters, those concerns extend to affordability, as the significant energy requirements of

new data centers raise questions about potential pressure on electricity costs.
Strap In for The Ride. Leading up to the midterm election, markets tend to experience
greater swings as investors navigate uncertainty surrounding the policy path for the
remainder of the presidential cycle (Exhibit 2A). Since 1930, the S&P 500 has averaged an
intrayear drawdown of 20% in midterm years, with the latest election in 2022 experiencing
a 25% intrayear pullback. This year, however, the S&P 500 largest peak-to-trough drawdown

has been 9%, considerably shallower than its long-term average (Exhibit 2B). Yet while
volatility may rise as election day approaches, 2026 has increasingly defied the traditional
midterm year playbook.
Why 2026 is Different. Despite entering a challenging stretch of the presidential cycle,
U.S. Equities have materially outpaced the typical year two pattern (Exhibit 2C). The S&P
500 experienced its best Q2 in a midterm year since 1938, surging 14.9% compared to an

average decline of 1.6%. From a monthly perspective, September stands out as the weakest
month, though month-to-date performance points to an unusual 1.0% rise. That resilience
follows a 2.6% gain in August, another month in which performance meaningfully exceeded
the historical decline of 0.5% (Exhibit 2D).
Through September, U.S. Equity resilience has also extended beyond headline returns with
both the frequency and magnitude of market pullbacks remaining relatively subdued. Market

corrections of 10% or more have occurred in just 29% of midterm election years since 1930.4
Against this backdrop, the market’s last correction came in spring 2025, while this year has
2 Bloomberg, Bureau of Labor Statistics. Data through August 31, 2026.
3 U.S. Treasury, Bureau of Economic Analysis. Data as of September 21, 2026.
4 Bloomberg, data through December 31, 2022.
Investment Implications
In our view, any related market
weakness could represent a

potential buying opportunity for
long-term investors.

### Page 5

5 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
seen only one 5%+ pullback compared to the average three per year, underscoring relatively
resilient market conditions.5 Year-to-date, the S&P 500 has increased 13%, well above the
average annual return of 3% according to Bloomberg through September 25, and on pace
to mark the strongest midterm election year since 2006. Taken together, 2026 has thus far
defied the traditionally weak midterm year seasonal pattern, reinforcing that every election

cycle is unique and historical trends may be a useful guide to navigate market volatility.
What’s Ahead? Forward performance is increasingly constructive for Equities. In the
post-war period, the S&P 500 rose 100% of the time in the six and 12 months following
elections, averaging 13% and 14%, respectively. 6 Even narrowing the time frame to three
months, markets ended higher 90% of the time. As investors push through the finish line,
the last three months of the year tend to be the strongest with October standing out,

ending higher 67% of the time. On average, Q4 has gained 5.6% since 1930, far surpassing
the preceding three quarters of election years. 7 Greater clarity about the U.S. fiscal policy
path serves as a potential tailwind for investors following the election.
To Take Home. Volatility tends to rise during midterm years, but history suggests that
reacting to short-term political uncertainty has rarely rewarded long-term investors. Over
longer horizons, the data suggests Equity returns and economic strength are shaped less

by election outcomes than by corporate profits, valuations, labor market conditions and
capital investments.

6 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THE TICKER TAPE
What is the bond market telling us?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investment Office
We believe the bond market is telling us that the Fed is on track to meeting their dual
mandate of price stability and full employment. More importantly, when examining the
full yield curve the 2-year Treasury yield is currently around 4.88%, which typically is
the most sensitive to the fed funds rate and tends to price in future potential policy

adjustments. At the same time, the 10-year Treasury yield is currently about 5.11%. This
leads us to believe that the market expects the Fed to engineer a couple of more hikes
while the back end of the curve is indicating that economic growth is still healthy.
Taken together, it appears the full curve, which has clearly flattened, believes the Fed is
doing its job, while supply shocks, for obvious reasons, are not leading to higher long-run
inflation. The backup in long-dated yields is growth, not necessarily inflation as indicated

by rising “real” yields. Therefore, we view the drift higher in yields as a more normal sign
than what many have originally suggested. We continue to emphasize higher economic
growth for longer, rising productivity in the medium term, supply shortages to remain in
key areas within the AI space, and shocks in the energy equation. We expect the energy
shocks to ultimately fade away over time, but we do not believe that growth fades
anytime soon. In our view, a more normal bond market pricing in Fed moves at the short

end, and growth at the long end helps financial conditions and economic growth operate
in a more durable manner.
In summary, the front-end of the curve expects 3 to 4 more hikes in the next 18 months
or so. This can be debated obviously based on future data especially short-term energy
supply shocks. Long-end yields are drifting higher based on five main factors below, in
our view.
1. Strong economic growth (both
services and manufacturing)
2. Hawkish Fed

3. Debt and deficit concerns
4. Lower demand for long duration
sovereign debt in general
5. Energy supply shocks
The largest weight being strong economic activity which kick starts portfolio
repositioning particularly as the Fed hikes rates. The repositioning stops once demand
rises again when yields reach attractive levels for the more patient longer term
investment community. The supply shocks ultimately end and debt and deficit worries will
likely remain, but they still take a backseat for now. Longer duration government debt is

becoming more attractive each week even in the face of the concerns above. Be patient

Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

S&P 500 Index Price Return is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States. A price return index is a stock
market index that tracks only the price changes and capital gains or losses of its component securities, without including dividends or cash distributions.

## Document 310: Consumer Checkpoint_ Strength in numbers September saw solid spending across many categories and a strong start to the holidays. 2 min read

Source PDF: all report pdf/Consumer Checkpoint_ Strength in numbers September saw solid spending across many categories and a strong start to the holidays. 2 min read.pdf

Economy 
Consumer Checkpoint: Strength in numbers 
09 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Consumer spending remained very solid in September. Bank of America aggregated credit and debit card spending per 
household rose 6.2% year-over-year (YoY), or 5.1% excluding gasoline, both the third strongest growth rates over the past four 
years. Month-over-month (MoM) spending was more muted, especially without gas. 
• Spending shows considerable breadth: the vast majority of discretionary retail and services categories are showing growth. 
Moreover, this strength extends across income cohorts, with the ongoing recovery of lower- and middle-income households' 
spending growth. 
• With the winter months fast approaching, holiday spending is already increasing at a solid rate, though it's still early. In Bank of 
America deposit data through September, buy now, pay later (BNPL) usage is rising but not at a rate that is overly concerning. 
However, nearly one in every five households that use BNPL has a repayment of over $500.

Consumer Checkpoint is a regular publication from Bank of America Institute. It aims to provide a holistic and real-time estimate of US 
consumers’ spending and their financial well-being, leveraging the depth and breadth of Bank of America proprietary data. Such data is not 
intended to be reflective or indicative of, and should not be relied upon as, the results of operations, financial conditions or performance of 
Bank of America. 
A solid September for spending   
Consumer spending continued to look solid in September. Bank of America total aggregated credit and debit card spending per 
household rose 6.2% year-over-year (YoY), while spending excluding gasoline increased 5.1% YoY. Notably, both are the third 
strongest growth rates in the past four years. Seasonally-adjusted total card spending rose 0.2% month-over-month (MoM), 
while spending excluding gasoline was little changed. Overall, spending growth remains well above the 2025 average rate 
(Exhibit 1). 
Exhibit 1: Total card spending per household rose 6.2% YoY and 5.1% YoY excluding gasoline in September

Most spending categories are growing  
One striking thing about recent card spending, in our view, is that it is well balanced across categories. For example, both 
spending on retail (excluding gasoline) and services grew strongly YoY in September, at 3.5% and 5.4%, respectively. Meanwhile, 
retail spending is showing notably better momentum than in 2025 (Exhibit 2).  
Exhibit 2: Spending is rising across retail and services 
Retail (excluding gasoline) and services card spending per household (monthly, seasonally adjusted, index January 2025 = 100)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Well balanced across “nice-to-have” and “need-to-have” retail categories  
Is the momentum in retail spending reflective of “nice-to-have” discretionary or “need-to-have” necessity spending? In retail, 
this is a nuanced question, as many categories sit in a gray area between “necessity” and “discretionary”. General merchandise 
stores, for example, sell both essentials like groceries as well as nice-to-have electronics or even jewelry.  
In Exhibit 3, we account for this by grouping retail spending into “more discretionary” (furniture, electronics, building materials, 
online retailers, clothing retailers and miscellaneous stores), “partly discretionary” (auto, health and personal care retailers, 
general merchandise) and “mostly necessity” (gas and groceries). Even within these categories the edges will be blurred, but the 
overall impression is that retail spending growth is widely distributed, with spending rising across all fronts. One caveat: “mostly 
discretionary” spending has been making a smaller contribution since June. 
Exhibit 3: Retail spending is distributed across discretionary and necessity categories  
Percentage points contribution to YoY growth by type of retail spending (three-month moving average)

Source: Bank of America internal data. See methodology for category aggregation definitions.  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Looking at the share of “more discretionary” retail spending categories that are increasing YoY underscores that growth is well 
balanced and broad-based (Exhibit 4). Non-store and online retailers and miscellaneous stores saw some of the fastest growth, 
while furniture and building materials were among the laggards, though other durable categories showed some strength.

For services, growth is driven mostly by discretionary spending  
In services spending, on the other hand, the boundaries between discretionary and necessity spending are often clearer. Travel 
spending (e.g., airlines, lodging) can be thought of as, by and large, discretionary. At the same time, utility bills, rent and 
insurance payments are necessities.  
And in services, discretionary spending remains the clear driver, accounting for over three-quarters of YoY spending growth in 
September (Exhibit 6). Leisure and housing services are major components of growth, though travel and restaurants also made 
positive contributions. However, as we noted last month strength in airline spending largely reflects price rises rather than 
transactions growth (read more in Consumer Checkpoint: Still sizzling).  
Exhibit 6: Services spending growth has been mostly driven by discretionary spending over the past year

Lower-income households’ spending outpaced all but the top 5% 
The breadth in spending growth across categories is also evident across income cohorts. In September, lower-income 
households’ total card spending growth was 5.5% YoY, while the equivalent figures for middle- and higher-income households 
were 4.9% and 4.8% YoY, respectively. The top 5% of households by income continue to show the fastest spending growth, 
5.9% in September, though this has eased somewhat (Exhibit 7). 
Overall, the divergences across income cohorts are much smaller than they were in 2025 and the first three months of this year. 
Just focusing on spending excluding key necessities (gasoline, food and utilities) doesn’t meaningfully change the picture of 
convergence across income cohorts (Exhibit 8).  
Exhibit 7: The top 5% saw the strongest spending growth of any 
income cohort… 
Total card spending per household by income tercile and the top 5%

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

The strength in lower- and middle-income households’ discretionary spending is occurring despite necessity spending shifting 
from a tailwind to a headwind over the past year amid rising gas prices (Exhibit 9). In our view, a key component of why these 
households have been able to combine rising necessity spending with strength in discretionary is the labor market and a 
recovery in their after-tax wage growth (Exhibit 10).  
In September, lower-income wage growth eased to 4.5% YoY from 4.7% YoY in August, while growth for middle- and higher-
income households rose to 3.8% and 3.7% YoY, respectively. These changes were modest, and lower-income households are 
continuing to see faster after-tax wage growth than other cohorts, a notable turnaround from the “K-shaped” pattern we 
observed over much of 2025 and 2026.

Should we be concerned about the easing in household spending and after-tax wage growth for those with the top 5% of 
incomes? For now, probably not, as in our view this spending growth is likely being driven by wealth effects rather than wage 
gains. Absent a sharp correction in equity markets, there is little reason to expect them to retrench their spending. However, 
developments here are worth watching as this cohort comprises a significant proportion of overall consumer spending. They also 
tend to make outsized contributions to several discretionary spending categories (Exhibit 11).  
Exhibit 11: The top 5% of households by income make up a disproportionate amount of spending in highly discretionary categories

With summer gone, thoughts turn to the holidays and BNPL… 
Now that summer is over, the next big gauge of consumer momentum is how people spend during the run-up to, and over, the 
holiday season. It is early days, but September spending on holiday items ran well ahead of 2025 (Exhibit 12). Given the backdrop 
of firmer after-tax wage growth and breadth across retail and services categories, this is perhaps not surprising. However, some 
of this spending strength could be due to rising prices as opposed to more purchases.

A concern often raised as we move into the holidays is whether consumers will lean on less traditional forms of credit such as 
buy now, pay later (BNPL) to finance their spending. Frequent BNPL users tend to have higher credit card utilization rates (read 
more in: Do consumers have wiggle room to absorb higher gas prices?).  
Looking at the share of households in Bank of America deposit data with a BNPL payment, we do tend to see a seasonal rise 
over the holidays (Exhibit 13), though in past years, these increases have quickly reversed. Further, while the underlying trend in 
BNPL usage is upwards, there are no signs of a sharp acceleration. We do, however, see some evidence that BNPL is being used 
to finance more expensive purchases, with the share of households making repayments above $500 rising, albeit relatively slowly 
(Exhibit 14).  
Exhibit 13: 8.3% of Bank of America households had a BNPL 
payment in September

(Exhibit 16). Overall, in our view, the increase in the share of BNPL users heading into the season does not appear too 
concerning, though the rate of usage for some BNPL consumers does bear watching.

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
The differences between the total and per household card spending growth rate (if discussed) can be explained by the following reasons:  
1. Overall total card spending growth is partially boosted by the growth in the number of active cardholders in our sample. This could be due to 
an increasing customer base or inactive customers using their cards more frequently. 
2. Per household card spending growth only looks at households that complete at least five transactions with Bank of America cards in the 
month. Per household spending growth isolates impacts from a changing sample size, which could be unrelated to underlying economic 
momentum, and potential spending volatility from less active users. 
3. Overall total card spending includes small business card spending while per household card spending does not. 
4. Differences due to using processing dates (total card spending) versus transaction date (per household card spending). 
5. Other differences including household formations due to young adults moving in and out of their parent’s houses during COVID. 
Any household consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level. Whenever median household savings and 
checking balances are quoted, the data is based on a fixed cohort of households that had a consumer deposit account (checking and/or savings 
account) for all months from January 2019 through the most current month of data shown.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Store category aggregation for “more discretionary”, “partly discretionary”, and “mostly necessity” is defined as follows: 
More discretionary: furniture, electronics, building materials, hobbies, miscellaneous stores, nonstore retail, clothing. 
Partly discretionary: auto, health and personal care, general merchandise. 
Mostly necessity: Gasoline, food and beverage.  
We consider a measure of services necessity spending that includes but is not limited to groceries, utilities, and gasoline. Discretionary services 
includes but is not limited to charitable donations, leisure travel, entertainment, and professional/consumer services. Discretionary retail 
includes but is not limited to general merchandise, miscellaneous, clothing, electronics and furniture. It excludes categories like groceries and 
gasoline. Holiday spending is defined as items in which spending in the November-December period is usually at least 20% of total annual 
spending on the category.  Value and premium grocers are determined judgmentally on a proprietary analysis of merchants by equity analysts. 
The “more discretionary” retail category includes furniture, electronics, building materials, online marketplaces, clothing retailers and 
miscellaneous stores; while “partly discretionary” retail contains auto parts and services, health and personal care retailers, general merchandise 
and “mostly necessity”  retail contains gas and groceries. 
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Any reference to card spending per household on gasoline includes all purchases at gasoline stations and might include purchases of non-gas 
items. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

## Document 352: Gridlock Won & Markets Cheer

Source PDF: all report pdf/Gridlock Won & Markets Cheer.pdf

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: Gridlock Won & Markets Cheer
Date:    6 hours ago
Gridlock Won & Markets Cheer

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
Gridlock Won & Markets Cheer
By Ed Yardeni & Elias Griepentrog • 4 Nov 2026
View in browser

I. Why Wall Street Is Cheering the Blue Sweep
Ahead of Tuesday's midterm elections, we warned that a Democratic
sweep of Congress could deliver a cold shower to Wall Street. It still could,
but it didn't do so today. Democrats captured both chambers of Congress,
flipping seven Republican-held Senate seats, including historic victories in
Texas, Kansas, and South Carolina. Financial markets put a positive spin
on the blue wave.
Oil prices fell on hopes that the US and Iran are more likely now to resolve
their war diplomatically. If so, then the outlook for inflation will improve. So
the Fed might not have to raise the federal funds rate as much. The

### Page 2

federal deficit might not widen further if Democrats refuse to increase
defense spending, while President Donald Trump vetoes their attempts to
provide Medicare for all. If Democrats slow the AI buildout, hyperscalers'
profits and free cash flow will increase. Fewer AI-related bond issues
would also help keep a lid on bond yields.
Initially, Nasdaq-100 futures plunged as much as 2.4% overnight, while
S&P 500 futures fell 1.4%. But the selloff reversed after Trump struck a

surprisingly conciliatory tone, congratulating Democrats and saying he
was willing to work with them.
That is when the cheer on Wall Street started. The Dow surged 3.0%
today, the strongest post-midterm rally since 1982. The S&P 500 jumped
1.6%, its best session in three months. The Nasdaq-100 advanced 0.9%.
The relief rally extended beyond equities. The 10-year Treasury yield fell
to 4.90%, back in what we have called the "old normal" range of
4.00%-5.00% (chart).

The dollar index (DXY) fell more than 1.5%, its biggest decline since
September. Gold rallied back above $4,000 an ounce. It might be finally
bottoming.

### Page 3

de-escalation in the Middle East
Brent crude plunged 8.7% to $94.62 a barrel amid hopes of Middle East
de-escalation. The nearby futures price of gasoline plunged to $2.73
(chart).
Investors may be warming up to the idea that some constraints on the AI
buildout could be healthy, as we have been arguing recently. Democratic
victories in key data center states, including Texas and Georgia, raise the
prospect of tighter construction restrictions amid concerns about electricity

and water consumption.
Hyperscalers rallied today, with Amazon and Microsoft gaining more than
6%, as capital discipline could ease concerns about AI investment returns
and improve free cash flow (chart). AI infrastructure providers struggled,

### Page 4

however. The PHLX Semiconductor Index fell 3.1% amid fears of weaker
demand from a slower buildout. We may be entering an AI Goldilocks
zone, with enough restraint to take some air out of the bubble without
derailing the boom.
II. The US Economy Continues To Perform Well
The economy and, therefore, earnings remain fundamentally resilient,
which continues to support the bull market. With the midterms behind us
and political uncertainty receding, investors can refocus on these strong

fundamentals. Here are the highlights of today's upbeat economic
indicators:
(1) ISM Services PMI. The ISM-NM PMI edged down modestly to 54.5 in
October, marking its 28th consecutive month of expansion (chart).
Underlying demand remained solid, with new orders and business activity
both comfortably above the 50.0 expansion threshold. Encouragingly, the
employment index rose from 50.1 to 51.4, marking its second consecutive
month of expansion.

### Page 5

Inflation is still a concern. The ISM prices-paid indexes remained elevated
in October (chart).
(2) Labor market. The average of the ISM employment indexes rose to
51.9 in October, its highest reading in roughly three years (chart). ADP
reported an 86,000 increase in private payrolls in October, beating
consensus expectations of 67,000.

### Page 6

(3) Trade balance. The latest trade data point to resilient US and global
economic growth. Merchandise imports rose 2.4% m/m in September to a
record $3.44 trillion annualized, while exports increased 2.5% m/m to
$2.09 trillion. The merchandise trade deficit widened to $1.35 trillion
annualized (chart).
Nominal and real US merchandise exports plus imports rose 21.1% y/y
and 10.1%, respectively, in September (chart). That suggests global trade
remains firmly in expansion territory and is consistent with strong global

economic growth.

### Page 7

Selected technology imports reached a record $61.3 billion in September,
reflecting the AI buildout’s heavy reliance on imported semiconductors,
servers, and other hardware (chart).

## Document 360: Inside the cost-of-living squeeze Households are weathering higher prices, but housing costs remain a key strain. 2 min read

Source PDF: all report pdf/Inside the cost-of-living squeeze Households are weathering higher prices, but housing costs remain a key strain. 2 min read.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-11-01
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

13039171     1

Economy 
Inside the cost-of-living squeeze 
04 November 2026

Key takeaways 
• Not every essential has become less affordable. Despite the cumulative rise in prices, households are spending a slightly smaller 
share of income on gas and groceries than they did several years ago. That suggests the affordability squeeze is less broad-
based than the inflation headlines alone might imply. 
• Housing is the major exception, and the pressure extends beyond lower-income households. Both mortgages and rent have 
increased since 2019. Some lower-income renters look particularly stretched, but around one in four higher-income mortgage 
holders also spend more than half their income on housing. 
• Wage gains and changing behavior have helped households absorb higher prices. But real wage growth has mostly softened, 
which may help explain why sentiment remains weak even as consumer spending continues to grow. In fact, declining sentiment 
likely reflects the sacrifices consumers have to make in order to keep spending.

Everyday essentials take up less income than they did four years ago 
Higher prices are still top of mind for consumers, especially when it comes to affordability. But just how stretched are 
households? Using Bank of America internal data, we look at how much of their income households are spending on everyday 
essentials like gas, groceries, mortgages and rent, and how that has changed over the past four years across income groups. We 
also explore how wage growth and shifts in spending habits have helped households cope with higher prices, and why consumers 
still feel downbeat even as they keep spending. 
For example, while consumers overall spent slightly more of their income on gas and groceries compared to last year, outlays for 
these necessities are still below what they were four years ago, according to Bank of America payment and deposit data (Exhibit 
1). Combined, gas and groceries accounted for nearly 13.5% of income in 2026, 10 basis points more than last year, but almost 
half a percentage point less than four years ago. And even with price increases over the past year, most consumers only spent 
3.2% of their income on gasoline, up from 2.9% a year ago and below the 3.4% from four years ago.  
Exhibit 1: Consumers overall spent slightly more of their income on gas and groceries compared to last year, though spending on these necessities 
is still below pre-pandemic levels

Across income groups, each cohort follows a similar pattern, though lower-income households do allocate the highest share of 
their income to gas and groceries (Exhibit 2).  
Exhibit 2: All income cohorts have experienced an increase in gas spending as a share of income, offsetting a decrease in grocery spending in 2026 
Median gas and grocery spending as a share of income by household income tercile (annual, %)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Housing stress isn’t just a low-income story 
Housing is an altogether different picture. Whether you’re a homeowner or a renter, housing is often the biggest expense of any 
consumer, accounting for around 30% of income. Median mortgage payments have steadily risen over the past two years and 
now account for more than 30% of a homeowner’s income, according to Bank of America internal data (Exhibit 3). 
While renters have experienced some easing, they tend to set aside more of their income to housing costs (Exhibit 4). Rents as a 
share of income peaked in 2025, with renters spending more than 32% of their income on shelter. Even so, a small improvement 
helps. Due to declines in housing payments, renters have seen a significant improvement in discretionary spending over the past 
year (read more in On the move: Renovation over relocation?). Also, some housing related costs have also eased in the past year 
(read more in Energy bills: Short-term relief, longer-term challenges and Insurance costs hit the brakes).  
Exhibit 3: Mortgage payments as a share of income have risen over 
the past two years… 
Median mortgage payments as a share of median income (annually, %)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
Note: Includes both internal (BofA) and external (non-BofA) mortgage payments.  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Exhibit 4: …while rent payments have eased over the same time 
period 
Median rent payments as a share of median income (annually, %)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Also, when it comes to housing, higher-income families typically spend more of their wages on mortgages than lower-income 
households (Exhibit 5). The converse is true for renters, with lower-income families spending around 35% of their paychecks on 
rent, five percentage points more than higher earners (Exhibit 6).

Some financially stretched households may be particularly affected by rising housing costs 
Notably, in the first nine months of the year, roughly one in four lower-income renters spent more than half of their income on 
rent, up from about one in five in 2019 (Exhibit 7). One positive note, the share of renters who spend most of their income on 
rent does seem to have declined slightly in 2026.  
Financial pressure from housing is not limited to lower-income households. Around one in four higher-income mortgage holders 
spend more than half of their income on home payments (Exhibit 8). And while only slightly more than one in five lower-income 
mortgage holders spent half of their income on housing, this is a nearly five percentage point increase compared to pre-
pandemic levels.

Wage growth and changing habits are insulating some households from inflation 
Still, some families have not seen their bills, as a share of income, rise alongside inflation. Why is this? In our view, this is likely 
because after-tax wages have increased significantly for these people. This is especially true for some lower-income households 
that have landed steady employment since the pre-pandemic era, according to Bank of America internal data and the Consumer 
Price Index (CPI) (Exhibit 9).  
Additionally, consumers have adjusted their behavior in response to higher prices. For one, we may be driving less than before 
the pandemic. Despite some increases in vehicle miles driven per capita over the past four years, we’re still getting behind the 
wheel less on average compared to 2019 levels, according to the US Census Bureau and Bureau of Transportation (Exhibit 10). 
At the same time, some households have “traded down” to less expensive stores or items and services (read more in The rise of 
value).  
Exhibit 9: Some lower-income households have seen their wages 
grow faster than inflation since 2019 
After-tax wages, based on Bank of America deposit data (three-month 
moving average, index 2019 = 100) compared to CPI inflation, based on 
Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) data (monthly, index 2019 = 100)

Source: Bank of America internal data, BLS 
Note: Data not available for October 2025 due to federal government shutdown. 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Exhibit 10: Vehicle miles driven per capita have not returned to pre-
pandemic levels 
Total vehicle miles traveled per total resident and civilian population 
estimates (monthly, index 2017 average = 100)

Source: US Census Bureau, Bureau of Transportation   
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Inflation is driving households to make more sacrifices 
There is one caveat to the affordability picture: wage growth among lower-income households slowed significantly throughout 
2025, though they’ve recovered since then. Furthermore, some middle- and higher-income families have not seen their pay keep 
up with inflation, at least compared to 2019 levels.  
In fact, using the ratio of after-tax wages to inflation as a proxy for “real wages,” we can see they largely declined throughout 
2022 and then again since mid-2023, according to Bank of America internal and BLS inflation data (Exhibit 11). Additionally, the 
drop in real wages is largely mirrored by a decline in the University of Michigan’s Index of Consumer Sentiment. This suggests 
that people have been feeling worse about their finances and the broader economy as their purchasing power has declined. 
Consumers are spending more than they did last year (read more in Consumer Checkpoint: Strength in numbers), but sentiment 
likely reflects the sacrifices consumers have made in order to keep spending.  
Looking ahead, in our view, the outlook for affordability is relatively encouraging: wage growth remains firm and has picked up 
for lower-income houses. That said, there are risks. BofA Global Research notes the potential for gasoline and grocery prices to 
increase. And higher mortgage rates make it difficult for those looking to make the jump from renting to homeownership.

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
The differences between the total and per household card spending growth rate (if discussed) can be explained by the following reasons:  
1. Overall total card spending growth is partially boosted by the growth in the number of active cardholders in our sample. This could be due to 
an increasing customer base or inactive customers using their cards more frequently. 
2. Per household card spending growth only looks at households that complete at least five transactions with Bank of America cards in the 
month. Per household spending growth isolates impacts from a changing sample size, which could be unrelated to underlying economic 
momentum, and potential spending volatility from less active users. 
3. Overall total card spending includes small business card spending while per household card spending does not. 
4. Differences due to using processing dates (total card spending) versus transaction date (per household card spending). 
5. Other differences including household formations due to young adults moving in and out of their parent’s houses during COVID. 
Any household consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level. Whenever median household savings and 
checking balances are quoted, the data is based on a fixed cohort of households that had a consumer deposit account (checking and/or savings 
account) for all months from January 2019 through the most current month of data shown.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Grocery, as discussed in this analysis, includes spending at grocery stores, warehouse clubs and dollar/discount stores.  
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Any reference to card spending per household on gasoline includes all purchases at gasoline stations and might include purchases of non-gas 
items. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

► 隨人工智慧運算需求持續攀升 ，谷歌、亞馬
遜、臉書、微軟及OpenAI等企業巨頭積極發
展客製化晶片 (ASIC)，藉此提升運算效率並
優化成 本 。根據 凱 基投顧 引述產 業數 據預
估，2027年客製化晶片出貨量將年增 92%至
1,446萬顆 ，首度超 越繪圖處理器 (GPU)的
1,129萬顆 ，顯示AI晶片市場正由 GPU主導
逐步轉向多元化發展。
► 在客製化晶片方面 ，谷歌持續擴大自研張量
處理器 (TPU)的應用與對外供應 ，亞馬遜亦
積極拓展自研AI晶片(Trainium)的雲端運算服
務，預估兩者合計將佔 2027年客製化晶片出
貨量約80%。
► 隨客製 化晶 片需 求 快速增 加 ，相 關晶 片設
計、先 進製 程及 先 進封裝 需求可 望同 步提
升，涉及上述業務發展的台股供應鏈 (註)有機
會受到市場關注。
熱門議題

### Page 6

6
投資焦點
► 2024年全球工廠運作的工業機器人約 470萬
台，為10年前的 3倍，主要為韓國 、中國和
日本等製造業大國將其運用於汽車 、電子和
金屬加工等產業 ，並因而維持成本和品質優
勢，為競爭力重要來源之一 。雖然美國工業
機器人的普及程度仍低於亞洲主要製造業國
家，但近年積極尋求重建工業產能 、將關鍵
供應鏈遷回美國國內以及擴大半導體和製藥
生產，有可能讓美國逐步調整工業和製造層
面的策略方向。
► 傳統工業機器人與人形機器人在外觀設計與
應用場景上有明顯差異 。工業機器人通常部
署於高度標準化的環境 ，以執行範圍相對有
限且重複性高的特定作業為主 ，而人形機器
人則被設計用於多變的環境 ，並經常需要與
人類協同工作 ，因此需結合移動能力 、感知
能力與靈巧操作能力 ，才能夠執行多樣化任
務，而非受限單一功能。
工業機器人技術相對成熟且部署速度較快全球運作中的工業機器人數量穩定成長
工業機器人技術成熟且穩定成長，為製造業強國的重要競爭力來源
投資焦點

### Page 7

7
投資焦點人形機器人
► 人 形機 器 人 主 要由 結 合半 導 體 與AI 模 型的
「大腦」，並由感測器 、執行器 、線材、連
接器及輕量化電池驅動的機身所組成 ，可透
過感知環境資訊 、思考並進行協調的實體動
作。投行預估至2030年，執行器將佔整體人
形機器人成本超過 50%，為價值含量最高的
核心零組件 ，麥肯錫公司甚至認為執行器與
感測器可能成為未來供應鏈中的關鍵瓶頸。
► 機器人在實體世界中執行任務時 ，任何延遲
或連線中斷都可能帶來重大風險 ，因此相較
純數位的 AI應用 ，對即時運算 (邊緣運算 )能
力的要求更高 。而近幾年邊緣運算軟硬體能
力大幅提升 ，使本地端設備得以部署數十億
參數的 AI模型 ，並將反應延遲縮短至約 0.3
秒，使機器人能夠即時感知 、決策與行動 。
此外，過去6年風險資本對機器人、感測器及
IT 硬體的每年平均投資約 100 億美元 ， 但
2026年僅上半年即已達 280億美元 ，暗示市
場對機器人產業潛力的期待有別於過往。
機器人技術進展快速並開始吸引風險資本投資人形機器人運作有賴AI模型與大量機身零組件整合

### Page 8

8
股市
◆ 美、歐、日央行升息抑通膨 ，公債殖利率走高壓抑股債市場近期動能 。然而包括油價自高檔回落使
通膨壓力稍緩 ，以及美股第三季獲利年增率仍為雙位數成長 ，股市表現有支撐 。市場持續觀察AI資
本支出是否帶來獲利成長 ，短期內評價面壓力將增添市場動盪 ，可分散配置於不同國家與產業 ，降
低市場波動影響 。總結上述，地緣衝突、殖利率偏高及評價面壓力等紛擾帶來負面影響 ，可布局防
禦股抗震，長期而言，科技及AI半導體發展趨勢不變且基本面佳，可逢拉回時分批布局。
◆ 歐、日股可作為分散配置重點 ，加上全球利率走揚有利銀行股 。此外，地緣衝突頻傳 ，歐洲國防軍
工仍有投資題材。
策略風格：短期可增加防禦股配置，中長
期看好AI題材如科技及半導體，非AI部分
可關注金融產業
區域：日本銀行股、歐洲金融、歐洲國防
債市
◆ 全球公債殖利率攀升 ，且最新聯準會利率點陣圖顯示 ，官員預估今年底前仍有一碼升息空間 ，立場
偏向維持較高利率水平抑制通膨發展 ，加上9月製造業及服務業PMI優於預期，殖利率偏高環境建議

以中短天期債券投資為主 ，可逢利率走升時布局進行鎖利領息 。高信評債體質較佳 ，具穩定現金流
與還款能力，聚焦風險調整後利差具吸引力者，包含金融、科技、通訊及公用事業等產業。
◆ 可以非美貨幣債券如歐元或澳幣計價投資級債券，分散單一貨幣風險。
債種：中短天期高信評債鎖利，產業如金
融、科技、通訊及公用事業等
衛星配置：非美貨幣債券分散風險
匯市
◆ 聯準會升息且態度偏鷹，美元高檔盤整。
◆ 日本銀行9月升息，但前瞻指引偏中性，未如市場預期的鷹派，日圓震盪。
美元高檔整理，日圓震盪

◆ 中東地緣衝突加劇推升油價，短期紛擾難解，偏高的油價可能對通膨及實體經濟帶來影響。
◆ 短期受美國雙率走高有壓 ，惟美日財政赤字壓力與去美元化使全球央行買盤依舊 ，提供黃金中長期
穩定支撐。

個別客戶因年齡、投資能力、金融商品交易經驗及風險承受度不同，適合之服務及/或金融商品亦有不同，應進一步與專屬理財業務人員進行評估。投資人應徹底瞭解相關服務及金融商品之風險 ，並審慎評估個人財力、資產狀況、
理財需求及風險承受能力，獨立判斷並自行決定是否投資及承擔有關風險。投資任何商品皆具有風險，商品以往之績效不保證未來之投資收益，本公司不負責商品之盈虧，也不保證最低之收益。
本公司保留對本文件及其內容之著作權及其他相關權利，任何人未經本公司同意，不得翻印或作其他任何用途使用(包括但不限於傳送或轉發予其他人士等)。如有違反情事，本公司除不負任何責任外，亦將對未經同意之使用與散布
等行為究責。
基金警語
投資一定有風險，基金投資有賺有賠，申購前應詳閱公開說明書
【信託產品投資須知及風險預告：（請投資人審慎評估下列各項條文內容）】
1. 基金（包括具有定期配息之基金）投資具投資風險，此一風險可能使信託本金發生全部虧損。
2. 基金委託人須自負盈虧，本公司受託投資不保本不保息，除盡善良管理人注意義務外，投資所可能產生的本金虧損、匯率損失、或基金解散、清算、移轉、合併等風險，均由委託人承擔。

3. 本公司受託投資之基金經金管會核准或同意生效，惟不表示絕無風險。基金經理公司以往之經理績效不保證基金之最低投資收益；基金經理公司除盡善良管理人之注意義務外，不負責基金之盈虧，亦不保證最低之收益，委託人
申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
4. 投資前應詳閱風險預告及各該基金公開說明書 ，確實作好財務規劃與風險評估 。本公司受託投資之基金已備有公開說明書 (或其中譯本)或投資人須知【前述文件併揭露有關基金應負擔之費用 (境外基金含分銷費用)】，委託人可
至本公司營業處所索取或至公開資訊觀測站、基金資訊觀測站或本公司網站下載。
5. 為保護既有投資人之權益，部份境外基金設有價格稀釋調整機制及公平市價規定，投資人申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書及投資人須知。
6. 委託人瞭解並同意受託人辦理特定金錢信託業務(即委託人委託投資基金)交易時，自交易相對人取得之報酬、費用、折讓等各項利益，得作為受託人收取之信託報酬；另委託人在持有基金期間 ，受託人仍持續收受經理費分成報
酬，委託人可至受託人(即本公司)網站 (https://www.kgi.com.tw/zh-tw/)查詢基金之通路報酬。其基金經理費率及其分成費率與其他通路報酬最新變動情形，亦可至該網站查詢。

7. 後收型基金在贖回時，基金公司將依持有期間長短收取不同比率之遞延申購手續費 ，該費用將自贖回總額中扣除；另後收型基金若有收取分銷費 ，則申購手續費雖可遞延收取，惟每年仍需支付0%~1.06%的分銷費（按基金日平
均淨資產價值計算），可能造成實際負擔費用增加（分銷費依各基金公司而有不同，請參考本公司後收型基金投資費用暨特約事項及各基金之投資人須知），另該費用已反映於每日基金淨值中，委託人無需額外支付。
8. 委託人之交易如經基金公司認定為短線交易者，基金公司或本公司可拒絕受理申請轉換或申購要求，並可請求支付一定比例之贖回費用或其他相關費用，費用標準均依各該基金公司之規定。
9. 委託人因不同時間進場，將有不同之投資績效，過去之績效亦不代表未來績效之保證。基金配息率不代表基金報酬率，且過去配息率不代表未來配息率；基金淨值可能因市場因素而上下波動，投資人於獲配息時，宜一併注意基
金淨值之變動。又投資新興市場可能比投資已開發國家有較大的價格波動及流動性較低的風險，委託人應慎選投資標的。
10. 部分基金可能持有衍生性商品部位，該部位可能超過基金淨資產價值之100%，並可能因而造成基金淨值高度波動及衍生其他風險。

11. 基金的配息可能由基金的收益或本金中支付。任何涉及由本金支出的部分，可能導致原始投資金額減損；且部分基金配息前未先扣除應負擔之費用。由本金支付配息之相關資料，投資人可至證券投資信託事業或總代理人之公司
網站查詢。
12. 非投資等級債券基金（本類基金主要係投資於非投資等級之高風險債券且配息來源可能為本金）（主要特色係投資前揭基金之組合基金亦同），適合能承受較高風險之『非保守型』委託人且不宜占投資組合過高的比重。由於非
投資等級債券之信用評等未達投資等級或未經信用評等，且對利率變動的敏感度甚高，可能會因利率上升、市場流動性下降，或債券發行機構違約不支付本金、利息或破產而蒙受虧損，不適合無法承擔相關風險之委託人；又如
係本國投信機構募集者，則可能投資於符合美國Rule 144A規定具有私募性質之債券，雖其投資總金額不得超過基金淨資產價值之百分之三十，然該債券具有債券發行人違約之信用風險、利率風險、流動性風險、交易對手風險
及因財務訊息揭露不完整或因價格不透明導致波動性較大之風險，投資人應審慎評估；另證券投資信託事業募集之債券型基金、平衡型基金、不動產證券化基金、以追蹤、模擬或複製標的指數表現之指數股票型基金（ETF）及

指數型基金或募集之基金投資新興市場國家債券達基金淨資產價值之百分之六十以上者，或有可能投資符合美國Rule 144A規定具有私募性質之債券，亦應留意相關風險。
13. 投資於中國之基金應遵守中華民國相關法令之規定：境外基金 (不含經金管會核准向證交所申請上市或交易之境外 ETF)如有投資大陸地區之有價證券者，以掛牌上市有價證券及銀行間債券市場為限 ，且投資前述有價證券總金額
不得超過該境外基金淨資產價值之百分之二十(境外基金機構依鼓勵境外基金深耕計畫向金管會申請並經認可者得適用鼓勵境外基金深耕計畫所列放寬前揭投資總金額上限至百分之四十之優惠措施 )；證券投資信託基金雖可投資
大陸地區之有價證券，惟仍應符合信託契約、公開說明書、證券投資信託基金管理辦法等相關規定及受到基金公司QFII額度之限制。又投資人須留意中國市場之政府政策、法令、會計稅務制度、經濟與市場等變動所可能產生之
投資風險。
14. 各基金於公開（含簡式）說明書或投資人須知揭露之風險報酬等級，係依據投信投顧公會「基金風險報酬等級分類標準」而訂定，本公司亦依據投信投顧公會「基金風險報酬等級分類標準」訂定基金產品風險等級。

富管理客服專線或透過財富管理客服信箱服務，查詢申訴案件處理進度。

## Document 416: The Institute Employment Report_ September 2026 Bank of America Institute analysis shows slower payroll growth, declining unemployment payments and continued strength in lower-inco

Source PDF: all report pdf/The Institute Employment Report_ September 2026 Bank of America Institute analysis shows slower payroll growth, declining unemployment payments and continued strength in lower-inco_223e3a6a.pdf

Economy 
The Institute Employment Report: September 2026 
07 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Job growth softened further in September, according to Bank of America's deposit data, with growth of 1.4% year-over-year 
(YoY), from 1.5% in August. But falling unemployment payments in September indicate a relatively healthy overall picture. 
• Lower-income households' wage growth remains strongest, despite a narrowing gap. After-tax wage growth eased to 4.5% YoY 
for lower-income households in September, while rising to 3.8% and 3.7% for middle- and higher-income households, 
respectively. 
• Lower tax withholdings and a relative strengthening in the younger/lower-income end of the labor market are driving this story. 
Additionally, average hours have risen in some lower-paid industries, which is also boosting pay packets.

Job growth slowed in September, but unemployment trends are benign  
Our estimate of job growth – which uses Bank of America deposit data to track changes in the number of customer accounts 
receiving a paycheck (see Methodology) – slowed further in September, to 1.4% year-over-year (YoY) from 1.5% YoY in August. 
The data can be noisy, partly due to seasonal variation and differences in pay-period timing, but in our view, the latest reading is 
consistent with the easing in payrolls growth seen in the September Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) data (Exhibit 1). 
Still, despite slower hiring, there is little sign of a rise in joblessness in Bank of America data. Unemployment payments into 
customer accounts showed a YoY decline in September (Exhibit 2). So, overall, labor market trends appear resilient.

After-tax wage growth cooled for lower-income, but rose for other cohorts 
Lower-income wage growth eased to 4.5% YoY in September from 4.7% YoY in August, while wage growth for middle- and 
higher-income households rose to 3.8% and 3.7% YoY, respectively. Overall, these changes were modest, and lower-income 
households are continuing to see faster after-tax wage growth than other cohorts, a notable turnaround from the “K-shaped” 
pattern we observed over much of 2025 and 2026 (Exhibit 3). 
Exhibit 3: Lower-income wage growth remains strongest, despite a narrowing gap 
After-tax wage and salary growth by household income terciles, based on Bank of America aggregated consumer deposit account data (three-month moving 
average, YoY%, SA)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
In our view, the pick-up in lower- and middle-income households’ after-tax wage growth continues to reflect the impact of the 
One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBA) in reducing tax withholdings, alongside an underlying rise in job-to-job changes at the 
younger/lower-income end of the labor market (for more, read: The Institute Employment Report: August 2026). Consistent with 
that underlying pick up, BLS data shows a solid YoY rise in average hours worked in several relatively lower-paid industries, such 
as manufacturing, retail and other services. Hourly workers in these industries are likely seeing rising paychecks as a result 
(Exhibit 4).

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Estimate of payroll growth from Bank of America internal data is based on the change in customer accounts receiving a paycheck in the month. 
An adjustment is made for the difference between overall population growth and customer account growth. Occasionally historic payments can 
be reclassified, leading to revisions in the series.  
An estimate of bonus growth from Bank of America deposit data is calculated by looking at customers who have received an inbound ACH 
payroll transaction in the last two years. From this sample an estimate of bonuses is derived by looking for payroll transactions which are over 
50% higher than the median regular payroll payments received by the customer. Of these payments only those that were received around the 
same time in each of the last two years are selected.  
The job-to-job change rate (j2j rate) is defined as the proportion of customers with an identified change in their employer as a proportion of 
the total number of customers with employment income. We estimate the median pay rise associated with a j2j change using the pay in the 
first three months of the new job compared to the same three months a year ago. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

## Document 501: ここが知りたい『投資信託販売チャネルの変化と現在地』

Source PDF: all report pdf/ここが知りたい『投資信託販売チャネルの変化と現在地』.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 1
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

投資信託販売チャネルの変化と現在地

資産運用調査部　主席研究員　大木 剛（おおき つよし）
　個人の資産運用において、 投資信託はNISAによる資
産形成やリタイアメン ト資金の運用などで、 重要性を増し
ている。 投資信託の販売では、 1998年12月の銀行窓販
解禁から四半世紀以上が経過した。 ここでは、 1999年以
降の公募投資信託の販売チャネル別の純資産額 （販売 ・
解約 ・ 時価変動が反映） と構成比率の推移を資料 １ で確認
したい。
　銀行窓販の解禁から1年後の1999年末時点で、 証券
は46.4兆円 （比率90％） 、 銀行等は2.8兆円 （同5％） とな
り、 その後も銀行等は預金取引等を背景とする顧客基盤
を活かし、 2007年末には34.0兆円 （同43％） まで比率
を高めた。 一方、 証券は45.3兆円 （同57％） と比率が低
下した。
　もっ とも、 銀行等の比率は2007～2008年をピークに
低下し、 証券の比率は上昇に転じた。 証券の純資産額に
は機関投資家も保有するETF （上場投資信託） が含まれ、
2010年代以降は日本銀行による大量買入れも統計上の
証券比率を大きく押し上げた。 資料 １ ・ ２ では、 個人向け投
資信託の動きをみるためETFを除いており、 それでも
2026年8月末時点では、 証券は159.9兆円 （同70％） 、 銀
行等は67.2兆円 （同29％） となっている。
投資信託の銀行窓販は証券にどれだけ迫ったか
　2009年以降の証券の比率上昇には、 証券各社の取組
みに加え、 いくつかの要因が考えられる。
　まず、 近年はNISAなどを活用した投資信託積立の拡大
により、 資産形成層を中心に大手オンライン証券会社の
利用が大きく増えている。
　また、 メガバンク等では、 顧客層に応じたグループ内の
銀行と証券の役割分担を強化し、 銀行顧客の富裕層や資
産形成層にもニーズに応じて、 証券がサービスを提供す
る動きが寄与している。
　さらに地域銀行において、 銀行の個人向け投資信託事
業を大手証券等との包括提携の下で共同運営する動き
も、 証券比率の上昇に作用している。
　一方で、 銀行等の純資産額も近年は堅調に伸びてお
り、 比率低下は、 銀行の落ち込みによるものではなく、 証
券の伸びが上回った影響が大きい。
　もっ とも、 「銀行と証券のどちらが高成長か」 という比較
より、 顧客ニーズに応じた役割分担の重要性が増してい
る。 2026年7月に政府が策定した 「金融戦略」 も、 インベ
ス トメン トチェーンの機能強化を掲げている。 多様な資産
運用ニーズに各金融サービスの担い手が強みを活かし
て応えることが、 資産運用立国の深化につながろう。

## Document 538: ユーロ圏ＧＤＰ（2026年7-9月期）－主要4か国はいずれも前期比伸び率が低下

Source PDF: all report pdf/ユーロ圏ＧＤＰ（2026年7-9月期）－主要4か国はいずれも前期比伸び率が低下.pdf
Report source: NLI
Date: 2026-11-02
Pages: 2
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1． 結果の概要：前期比 0.4％と高成長を記録 
10月30日、欧州委員会統計局（Eurostat）はユーロ圏ＧＤＰの一次速報値（Preliminary Flash 
Estimate）を公表し、結果は以下の通りとなった。 
【ユーロ圏21か国ＧＤＰ（2026年7-9月期、季節調整値）】 
・前期比は0.4％、市場予想1（0.2％）を上回り、前期（0.6％）から低下した（図表1・2） 
・前年同期比は1.3％、市場予想（1.1％）を上回り、前期（1.2％）から上昇した 
（図表 1）

（図表 2）

2． 結果の詳細：アイルランド要因を除くと前期比伸び率はやや減速 
ユーロ圏の26年 7-9月期の成長率は前期比0.4％（年率換算 1.7％）となった。4-6月期（前期
比 0.6％、年率 2.6％）から減速したが、高めの成長率を維持した。前年同期比の伸び率は 1.3％
（4-6月期 1.2％）だった。なお、近年はアイルランドがユーロ圏全体の成長率も大きく左右して
おり、アイルランドを除くユーロ圏の実質成長率（図表 1）は 7-9月期が前期比 0.2％（年率換算
0.8％） 、前年同期比 1.2％、4-6月期が前期比0.3％（年率換算 1.2％） 、前年同期比 1.3％であり、
成長基調は維持されているものの前期比での伸び率はやや減速している。 
経済規模の大きい4か国の伸び率を見ると、前期比ではドイツ 0.2％（4-6月期 0.3％）、フラン
ス▲0.1％（同0.0％）、イタリア0.0％（同 0.2％）、スペイン0.6％（同0.7％）となり（図表3横
軸）、これまで同様スペインが高成長を維持しているが、いずれの国でも成長率は低下した。特に足
もとではフランスの伸び悩みが目立つ。前期比伸び率について、ドイツ統計局は投資が減少する一

（前期 0.4％） 、投資▲0.6％（前期▲0.5％） 、輸出▲1.7％（前期2.8％） 、輸入0.1％（前期0.9％）
となった（図表5）。在庫変動の前期比寄与度は0.4％ポイント（前期▲0.7％ポイント）、純輸出の
前期比寄与度は▲0.6％ポイント（前期0.6％ポイント）だった。個人消費は改善したが、投資や輸
出の減少が全体の成長率を抑制した。産業別の付加価値伸び率は、第一次産業が▲1.4％（前期▲
3.2％） 、工業が▲1.1％（前期 0.2％） 、建設業が▲0.5％（前期▲0.9％） 、市場型サービス産業が
0.1％（前期0.1％） 、非市場型サービスが 0.1％（前期0.2％）だった。

## Document 544: 中共５中全会閉幕、次期党大会を見据えて党・軍への統制強化を加速 ～経済政策の実効性と日中関係の行方にも注目～

Source PDF: all report pdf/中共５中全会閉幕、次期党大会を見据えて党・軍への統制強化を加速 ～経済政策の実効性と日中関係の行方にも注目～.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-10-30
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Asia Trends

2026 年10 月 30 日

1/6 
中共５中全会閉幕、次期党大会を見据えて党・軍への統制強化を加速 
～経済政策の実効性と日中関係の行方にも注目～ 
 経済調査部 主席エコノミスト  西濵 徹 
（℡：050-5474-7495）

（要旨） 
○ 中国では、10 月26～29 日に中国共産党の第20 期中央委員会第５回全体会議（５中全会）が
開催された。今回は、従来の慣例とは異なり、「全面的かつ厳格な党内統治」が主要議題と
なり、党規律や幹部の監督、反腐敗の強化、党中央による政策履行の徹底などを盛り込んだ
決定が採択された。また、軍幹部を含む多数の党籍剥奪処分を追認するなど、党・軍に対す
る統制強化の姿勢が鮮明となった。さらに、2027 年後半に第 21 回党大会を開催することが
正式に決定され、習近平指導部は次期党大会に向けて組織・人事面での基盤固めを進める方
針を示したと考えられる。 
○ 一方で、経済政策面では、内需拡大や景気安定に向けた方針が再確認されたものの、具体的
な追加刺激策は示されなかった。足元の中国経済は、外需の堅調さを背景に持ち直しの動き
がみられる一方、不動産不況や消費・投資の低迷など、内需の弱さが依然として課題となっ
ている。こうしたなか、今後は既存の景気支援策の実効性が焦点となるほか、党内統治の厳
格化が行政の政策遂行能力を高めるのか、一方で幹部の責任回避などによる政策執行の遅れ
を招くのかが注目される。 
○ 日本への影響については、中国の内需回復が日本企業の輸出や現地販売を下支えする可能性
がある一方、 中国政府による国産化や産業育成の推進が競争圧力を強めることも懸念される。
さらに、重要部材の輸出管理強化や台湾情勢を巡る安全保障上の不確実性が、日本企業の調
達や事業活動に及ぼす影響にも注意が必要となる。 今後は、2027 年の次期党大会に向けた組
織・人事の動向に加え、景気支援策の具体的な執行状況、地方行政の対応、対外関係の変化
などを通じて、習近平指導部の政策運営の方向性を見極める必要がある。

【今回の５中全会では、党内統治の強化と政策の履行徹底が主要議題となった】 
中国では、10 月 26 日から 29 日までの日程で中国共産党の第 20 期中央委員会第５回全体会議
（５中全会） が開催された。党大会の閉会期間中に党の指導機関としての役割を担う中央委員会は、
５年の任期中にほぼ１年ごとに１回全体会議を開催し、 党の統治や運営上の重要政策、 人事などの
議題を討議する。１中全会では新指導部が正式に発足し、２中全会では国務院（政府）などの人事

### Page 2

2/6 
案を承認し、３中全会では経済政策を討議するなど、２中全会と３中全会は通常、党大会の翌年に
開催される。 しかし、 習近平指導部は３中全会を2023 年に開催せず、2024 年７月に後ずれした（注
１）。さらに、2025 年 10 月に開始した４中全会では、第 15 次５ヵ年計画（十五五）が討議された
（注２）。 
その結果、通常は５ヵ年計画を討議する場となる５中全会で、今回は「全面的かつ厳格な党内統
治 （全面従厳治党） 」 が主要議題となり、 実質的に党大会の前年に開催される６中全会に近い性格を
帯びることとなった。このように、習近平指導部の下では、党の会議の開催時期や議題などについ
て、過去の慣例にとらわれない動きがみられる。 
会議では、 習氏が中央政治局を代表して行った活動報告を聴取 ・ 討議するとともに、 「全面的かつ
厳格な党内統治を持続的に推進するうえでの若干の重大問題に関する決定」 を採択した。同決定に
は、党規律、幹部の選抜・監督、組織運営、反腐敗の強化といった内容が盛り込まれた。会議後に
公表された公報によれば、 中央の指示を地方や各部門まで徹底するとともに、 政治的な忠誠と実務
上の遂行能力を幹部管理に反映させる姿勢がうかがえる。 反腐敗を長期的な執政の基盤と位置付け
たうえで、 個々の処分にとどまらず、 組織的な監督と責任追及の仕組みを整える狙いがあると考え
られる。 
この方向性を考えるうえでは、会議に先立って党の理論誌『求是』に掲載された習氏の演説が重
要な手がかりとなる。これは１月に行われた演説の主要部分を公表したものであり、 このなかで習
氏は、党中央の決定が実行されない、または恣意的に実行される問題を挙げるとともに、政治的な
監督の強化の必要性を訴えた。このため、今回の会議においても、政治的忠誠と政策履行の徹底、
反腐敗と監督の制度化、幹部の評価・登用の厳格化が重視されたと考えられる。 
人事面では、中央軍事委員会の前副主席である張又侠氏や元委員の劉振立氏など 33 人に対する
党籍剥奪処分を追認したほか、 別の１人に対する党内職務の罷免処分を決定した。 党籍剥奪の対象
者は、 習近平指導部の下で開催された中央委員会全体会議として過去最多の規模とみられ、 うち16
人は元軍関係者が占めた。軍幹部を巡っては、ここ数年の相次ぐ処分が指揮命令系統の弱体化を招
くと懸念されてきた。このため、中央軍事委員会の欠員補充が注目されたものの、今回は同委員会
副主席の張昇民氏を中央政治局委員に追加選出するにとどまった。軍の規律検査を担ってきた張氏
を昇格させることで、 次期党大会に向けて軍への統制を強化し、 指導体制の立て直しを進める方針
が示されたと考えられる。 とはいえ、 軍の指揮命令系統を巡る空洞化の問題は残されたままである。
一方、 今回は中央委員24 人が党籍剥奪処分の対象となるなか、 中央候補委員10 人を中央委員に繰
り上げるなど、組織体制の補強も図られた。こうした動きを踏まえれば、今回の５中全会は党・軍
への引き締めを一段と強化する会議となったと捉えられる。

注１ 2024 年７月 19 日付レポート「中国･３中全会､習近平氏が掲げる｢中国式現代化｣の加速を推進」 
  （https://www.dlri.co.jp/report/macro/351237.html） 
注２ 2025 年10 月24 日付レポート「中共４中全会閉幕､中長期的な国力向上の姿勢が鮮明に」 
  （https://www.dlri.co.jp/report/macro/530799.html）

### Page 3

3/6

【2027 年に開催予定の次期党大会に向けて、組織・人事面での基盤整備に注力】 
５中全会では、中国共産党第 21 回全国代表大会（第 21 回党大会）を 2027 年後半に北京で開催
することを正式に決定した。これにより、今後行われる中央・地方の幹部人事や政策運営を評価す
るうえでは、次期党大会に向けた準備という視点がこれまで以上に重要になる。 
５中全会での決定事項を踏まえれば、政治的忠誠と政策遂行能力の重視という観点が、 次の指導
部を担う幹部の評価と選抜に関わると考えられる。次期党大会に向けて地方・中央の人事が進めら
れる過程では、党中央の方針をどれだけ着実に実行できるかが問われることになろう。ただし、今
回行われた中央委員の補充は党規約に定められた手続きに沿ったものであり、今回補充された 10
人がそのまま後継指導者候補とはならないことに注意が必要である。 
さらに、思想面から現行指導体制の継続を支える動きも重要であろう。具体的には、公報におい
て、毛沢東思想や鄧小平理論など従来の指導思想に言及するなかで「習近平党建設思想」が明記さ
れ、習近平「新時代の中国の特色ある社会主義」思想の重要な構成要素と位置付けられたことは、
同指導部による党運営の理念を定着させる動きと捉えられる。 同思想は、 「人民の利益を重視する
（人民至上） 」立場を基本としつつ、党統治の厳格化、 反腐敗運動の徹底、 民主集中制の強化など、
今回の５中全会で討議された内容と重なる要素が多い。なお、この概念は 2026 年６月に開催され
た全国党建設活動座談会で正式に提起・体系化されており、今回初めて登場したものではない。今
後は、次期党大会の政治報告や党規約などにどのような形で反映されるかが注目される。 
前述したように、 ５中全会では多数の軍幹部が処分されたものの、これは規律による統制を徹底
する姿勢と、人事面での再整備を続ける必要性の双方を示唆している。今回、中央政治局委員に選
出された張升民氏は軍の規律検査を担ってきた経歴を有しており、習近平指導部が人民解放軍に対
する政治的統制を重視してきた姿勢と整合的である。 一方で、軍人事の変更だけで軍の実戦能力や
対外行動を直接判断することは難しい。 習氏は、 台湾統一を目的とする武力行使を選択肢から排除
しておらず、 米軍に対抗し得る『世界一流の軍隊』の建設を目指してきた。2027 年８月には人民解
放軍の創設100 周年を迎えることから、 それまでに軍の指揮命令系統の再構築が進めば、 同年後半
の党大会で４期目入りを目指すとみられる習氏にとって、 権力基盤の一層の安定につながる可能性
がある。

【経済政策面では具体策に乏しいものの、景気安定に向けた政策の実行力が焦点に】 
一方で、経済政策との関係では、十五五の策定過程との区別が必要である。十五五に関する「建
議」は４中全会で採択されるとともに、それを具体化した「綱要」は2026 年３月の全人代（第 14
期全国人民代表大会第４回全体会議）で承認されている （注３）。このため、今回の５中全会は、 こ
うした既存の政策を着実に遂行する組織・幹部の規律を整えるための会議と位置付けられる。

注３ ３月５日付レポート「中国､2026 年全人代開幕､特異性を強める経済､内政､外交」 
  （https://www.dlri.co.jp/report/macro/581059.html）

### Page 4

4/6 
こうしたなか、 公報では、マクロ政策の活用と内需拡大を求めるとともに、2026 年の経済・社会
発展目標を達成する方針を再確認した。一方で、追加財政支出の規模や利下げ幅、新たな家計給付
の金額など、具体的な政策措置は示されなかった。今後の政策運営の動きについては、政府や中国
人民銀行（中銀）をはじめとする金融当局が発表する政策の内容に加え、その執行状況などを確認
したうえで評価する必要があると考えられる。 
足元の中国経済を巡っては、経済成長率に持ち直しの動きがみられる一方、 内需は力強さを欠く
推移をみせている。 ７-９月の実質ＧＤＰ成長率は前年同期比＋4.5％と前期 （同＋4.3％） から小幅
に加速したほか、前期比年率ベースでは＋4.9％と試算されるなど持ち直しの動きが確認されてい
る（注４）。一方で、９月までの累計ベースの小売売上高（社会消費品小売総額）は前年比＋1.1％
にとどまり、固定資産投資は同▲7.2％とマイナスで推移するとともに、なかでも不動産投資は同
▲20.6％と大幅に下振れしている。 こうしたなか、 足元の景気は外需の旺盛な動きを追い風にした
ハイテク製造業を中心とする供給サイドがけん引役となる展開が続いており、国内の需給不均衡が
解消されない状況にある。国家統計局も、内需の弱さを経済運営上の課題として認識しており、景
気の回復力にはなお弱さが残ると考えられる。 
このため、 金融市場では、2027 年後半に開催される党大会を前に、 景気や雇用、 社会の安定を維
持する観点から、何らかの政策対応が強化されるとの見方が根強い。とはいえ、内需の低迷が長期
化しているにもかかわらず、 これまで大規模な景気刺激策に積極姿勢をみせてこなかったことを勘
案すれば、政治日程のみを材料に積極姿勢に転じると見込むことは難しい。国務院は７月、同国で
初めて個人消費に焦点を当てた 「消費拡大の５ヵ年計画」 を公表した。同計画では、2030 年の小売
売上高（社会消費品小売総額）の規模を2025 年（50.1 兆元）に比べて約２割（60 兆元前後）引き
上げるべく、消費構造の高度化や家計所得の引き上げを目指すとした。加えて、共産党は７月末に
開催した中央政治局会議において、 インフラ関連など財政支出の進捗加速による景気下支えを図る
方針を示した。これを受けて、政府・金融当局は８月以降、内需促進を目的とする新たな財政・金
融支援策を発表しており、今後はこれらの政策がどの程度の効果を上げるかを見定める必要がある。 
一方で、今後焦点となり得るのが、党内統治の厳格化と政策実行力の関係であろう。汚職や形式
的な行政対応が減るとともに、党中央・政府が提示した内需支援策が適切に執行されれば、政策の
実効性が高まることが期待される。ただし、幹部が責任追及を警戒して判断を先送りするほか、上
位機関への照会を増やすような動きが広がれば、 企業の許認可や政策資金の執行が遅れる可能性は
残る。 今回の決定のみをもって政策効果の行方を判断することはできず、 地方レベルでの行政実務
の動向を注視する必要があろう。その意味では、政策の規模に加え、資金の使途と執行状況を点検
することが重要になる。仮に、今後も供給サイドの改革が優先されるとともに、産業設備投資への
支援が集中すれば、需要に見合わない生産能力の増強を招くとともに、国内における価格競争や対
外摩擦を強める可能性に注意が必要となる。

注４ 10 月19 日付レポート「中国・７-９月GDP は前年比＋4.5％に加速も、外需依存の構図は変わらず」 
  （https://www.dlri.co.jp/report/macro/666711.html）

### Page 5

5/6

【日本への影響を巡っては、中国の内需動向、行政運営、産業競争、供給網などの動向に注意】 
日本にとって中国は、 需要先であると同時に重要な供給元でもある。2025 年時点において、日本
の貿易に占める中国の割合は、輸出で 17.0％、輸入で 23.6％となっている。中国の需要動向は日
本の輸出や現地販売に、供給面の変化は日本の調達や生産活動に影響を及ぼす。このため、５中全
会で示された党内統治の強化の行方が、今後どのような形で政策運営や行政実務に反映されるかが
重要になる。 
まず、内需の行方は、中国に進出する日系企業の販売機会に直結すると予想される。当局が主導
する家計所得や雇用への支援が実効性を持つことができれば、 消費関連の日系企業の現地販売のほ
か、 日本からの輸出を支えることが期待される。一方で、産業設備への投資が中心となる場合には、
機械・電子部品・素材などに需要が向かいやすい。ただし、支援対象、現地調達条件、日本企業の
競争力によって恩恵は異なると予想されるほか、 中国の景気対策の規模がそのまま日本の輸出増に
つながるとは限らない点には注意が必要である。 
次に、 行政運営が事業環境に及ぼす影響にも注意が必要となる。 規律強化が恣意的な行政や不透
明な取引を減らせば、企業の事業環境に対する予見性を高める効果が期待できる。反対に、担当者
の交代や責任追及への警戒が判断の遅れを招く場合には、事業認可、政府調達、契約履行などに支
障が生じ得る。中国日本商会が発表した 2026 年白書においても、行政手続の迅速化、制度運用の
統一、内外企業の公平な競争条件の確保などが求められている。日本企業にとっては、こうした改
善が実務レベルでどの程度進展するかが重要な判断材料になる。 
さらに、中国国内における産業育成に伴う需要と競争環境の行方にも注意が必要となる。習近平
指導部が掲げる科学技術の自立自強やサプライチェーンの強靱化、 「新質生産力」の育成といった
方針は、すでに十五五の策定段階で重視されている。今後、こうした動きが一段と強力に推進され
れば、日本の設備・部材メーカーに販売機会をもたらすことが期待される一方、国産化が進んだ製
品については中期的に競争圧力が強まる可能性がある。すでに、 太陽光電池や風力発電設備につい
ては、中国メーカーが世界市場で高いシェアを有するほか、ＥＶ（電気自動車）でも高い国際競争
力を持つ。中国市場における需要の行方だけでなく、日本企業の技術や製品が代替される可能性の
ほか、第三国市場での競争激化が一段と加速する可能性にも注意が必要である。さらに、世界的に
ＡＩ（人工知能） ・半導体を巡る開発競争が激化するなか、７月には中国が主導して世界ＡＩ機構
（WAICO） という国際機関を設立するなど、 この分野をリードする姿勢を強めており、 その動向にも
注意が必要である。 
足元で特に注視すべきなのが、 重要部材の供給と安全保障を巡る影響であろう。 このところの中
国は、高市首相による台湾有事を巡る国会答弁や日本の防衛政策に対する反発を強めるなか、日本
に対する 「経済的威圧」 を強めている。 中国商務部は１月､日本に対する軍民両用品（デュアルユー
ス）の輸出管理を強化する方針を発表し、その後も対象を拡大するなど、規制を強化してきた。足
元では、 レアアース関連の輸出許可や通関手続きの運用を巡る不確実性が、 企業実務上の重要な課
題となっている。今後も、対象品目や審査基準に関する当局の個別公告に加え、企業による輸出許

### Page 6

本資料は情報提供を目的として作成されたものであり、投資勧誘を目的としたものではありません。作成時点で、第一ライフ資産運用
経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。 
6/6 
可の取得や調達の動向を確認する必要があることは間違いない。 
安全保障面についても、 軍幹部の処分や政治局人事をもって、台湾有事の時期や可能性を判断す
ることは困難である。日本への影響については、台湾海峡や東シナ海における実際の軍事活動、日
中間の外交対話、経済措置の変化を通じて判断する必要がある。企業にとっては、物流、重要部材
の調達、 現地拠点の運営にどのような支障が生じ得るかを、 平時から具体的に点検することの重要
性がこれまで以上に高まっていると判断できる。

【今後は、政策の実施内容と現場レベルでの反応を確認する必要性が高まっている】 
先行きについては、 現行路線の継続と党大会に向けた景気安定重視が基本線となる一方、 行政の
実行力や対外関係の行方によって実際の展開が左右されると考えられる。とりわけ、 幹部人事と次
期指導部の形成過程、家計支援・不動産対策・産業支援の具体的内容と執行状況、日系企業の許認
可・代金回収・調達の実態、輸出管理や外交・安全保障上の具体的な変化を継続的に確認する必要
性は高まっている。次期党大会で示される政治報告と、 その後に開催される１中全会での指導部人
事の行方は、その先の政策運営を評価する重要な材料となる。その意味では、開催まで約１年とな
った次期党大会に向けて、 党中央と地方組織双方の動きをこれまで以上に注視する必要があると考
えられる。

## Document 545: 中国・７-９月 GDP は前年比＋4.5％に加速も、外需依存の構図は変わらず ～供給サイドが成長をけん引する一方、個人消費と不動産は_f9a4f0d8

### Page 1

Asia Trends

2026 年10 月 19 日

1/6 
中国・７-９月 GDP は前年比＋4.5％に加速も、外需依存の構図は変わらず 
～供給サイドが成長をけん引する一方、個人消費と不動産は力強さを欠く展開が続く～ 
 経済調査部 主席エコノミスト  西濵 徹 
（℡：050-5474-7495）

（要旨） 
○ 中国経済は、世界的なＡＩ・半導体需要を背景に輸出や製造業が堅調に推移する一方、不動
産不況や雇用回復の遅れを背景に個人消費や投資は力強さを欠いており、 「強さ」 と 「弱さ」
が混在している。７-９月の実質ＧＤＰ成長率は前年比＋4.5％と前期から加速し、堅調な外
需を追い風に、製造業、鉱業が景気を下支えする動きが確認された。ただし、企業部門では
商品市況上昇によるコスト増が進む一方、家計の節約志向から最終価格への転嫁は難しく、
企業部門のインフレと消費者部門のディスインフレが併存している。 
○ 政府は個人消費喚起策や財政 ・ 金融支援を強化しており、 ９月の小売売上高（前年比＋1.1％）
には持ち直しの兆しがみられた。ただ、その中身をみると、低価格業態やＥＣへの需要シフ
トが進む一方、 自動車や宝飾品、 娯楽関連などの需要は弱く、 家計の節約志向は依然根強い。
不動産市場も大都市の一部で改善の兆しがみられるものの、地方都市では住宅価格の下落が
続き、不動産投資（年初来前年比▲20.6％）は歴史的な低水準にある。一方、設備投資や公
共投資も含む固定資産投資（年初来前年比▲7.2％）には底打ちの兆しもみられる。 
○ 供給面では、 ９月の鉱工業生産が前年比＋5.5％と加速し、外需の旺盛な動きを追い風にした
ハイテク製造業を中心に堅調さが続いている。内需が弱いなか、中国経済は引き続き外需と
供給サイドへの依存度が高く、国内の需給不均衡も続いている。加えて、原材料価格の上昇
を製品価格へ十分転嫁できず企業収益が圧迫されていることや、不動産規制への懸念から中
国本土株は上値の重い展開が続いている。先行きについては、政策効果の及び方によって業
種間の業績格差が一段と広がる可能性には引き続き注意が必要と考えられる。

【外需の堅調さをけん引役に、７-９月ＧＤＰは前年比＋4.5％に伸びが加速している】 
足元の中国経済を巡っては、 世界的なＡＩ （人工知能） ・ 半導体関連需要の旺盛さを追い風に輸出
は堅調な動きをみせている。こうした動きを背景に、ＡＩ関連やＥＶ（電気自動車）など個別企業
は世界的な競争力を高めるなど、ミクロ面での強さがうかがえる。一方で、長引く不動産不況や若
年層を中心とする雇用回復の遅れなどが個人消費をはじめとする内需は力強さを欠いている。 この
ように、マクロ面でみた中国経済は弱さがうかがえるなど、相反する材料が混在している。

### Page 2

2/6 
こうした状況は、 足元の物価動向にも現れている。 イラン情勢の悪化を受けた原油をはじめとす
る商品市況の上昇を反映して、川上段階の企業部門はインフレに直面している。さらに、企業間取
引段階においては価格転嫁の動きが広がりをみせる流れも確認できる。 しかし、 家計部門が節約志
向を強めるなかで、 企業部門にとっては最終製品への価格転嫁のハードルが高い状況が続いている。
その結果、消費者段階の物価は低迷するディスインフレ基調が続いている。このように、足元の中
国経済はインフレとディスインフレが共存する展開となっている（注１）。 
このように、足元の中国経済は「強さ」と「弱さ」が混在する状況にあるなか、７-９月の実質Ｇ
ＤＰ成長率は前年同期比＋4.5％となり、 前期 （同＋4.3％） から加速した （図１）。 前期比の成長率
も＋1.2％と前期（同＋0.9％）から加速
しており、年率換算では＋ 4.9％と試算
されるなど持ち直しの動きが確認され
ている。分野ごとの動きをみると、個人
消費など内需の弱さを反映して第３次
産業（サービス業）は頭打ちする一方、
第１次産業（農林漁業）の生産は堅調に
推移しているほか、 第２次産業 （製造業 ・
鉱業） の生産が持ち直したことが足元の
景気を下支えしている。イラン情勢の悪
化による商品市況の上昇を受けて、 鉱業
部門の生産が活発化しているほか、 輸出の堅調さが製造業の生産を押し上げる展開が続く。 前述の
ように川上段階でのインフレを受けて、 名目成長率 （前年同期比＋6.0％） は２四半期連続で実質成
長率を上回る伸びとなるなど、マクロ的には「デフレ脱却」が進んでいる。

【個人消費に一見持ち直しの兆しも、その内容をみれば依然として厳しい状況が続いている】 
個人消費をはじめとする内需が力強さを欠くなか、国務院は７月、 同国で初めて個人消費に焦点
を当てた 「消費拡大の５ヵ年計画」を公表した。 同計画では、2030 年の小売売上高 （社会消費品小
売総額）の規模を2025 年（50.1 兆元）に比べて約２割（60 兆元前後）引き上げ、その実現へ消費
構造の高度化や家計所得の引き上げを目指すとした。 加えて、 共産党は７月末に開催した中央政治
局会議において、 インフラ関連など財政支出の進捗加速による景気下支えを図る方針を示した。 財
政部・中銀（中国人民銀行） ・国家金融監督管理総局は、８月17 日付で内需促進を目的とする新た
な財政・金融支援策を発表し、中小・零細民間企業や消費者向け融資への利子補給を拡充すること
を明らかにしている。 
こうした一連の個人消費喚起に向けた動きも追い風に、 ９月の小売売上高 （社会消費品小売総額）

（同▲0.16％） から２ヵ月ぶりの拡大に

りの高い伸びとなるなど、 持ち直しの動
